* A Patchwork Approach to AI Regulation Is Best—for Now
  Foreign Policy | 03 Aug 21:10 UTC
  Many want a global treaty to govern AI risk. California and New York may already be writing it.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. On July 16, roughly 200 delegates, including Nobel laureates and former heads of state and government, signed a declaration in Rome calling for broad global governance of artificial intelligence. Ten days earlier, in Chicago, Illinois, Gov. J.B. Pritzker signed a law requiring large frontier AI developers to submit to independent annual audits. Pritzker’s act received less fanfare—but it may ultimately matter more for the way AI risks are regulated around the world. The Illinois law joins earlier legislation passed in California and New York that, together, will impact every major AI developer in the United States. Collectively, these laws require AI companies to publicly document how they manage catastrophic risk, report safety incidents to state authorities, and, with Illinois’s new legislation, open their safety frameworks to external scrutiny. On July 16, roughly 200 delegates, including Nobel laureates and former heads of state and government, signed a declaration in Rome calling for broad global governance of artificial intelligence. Ten days earlier, in Chicago, Illinois, Gov. J.B. Pritzker signed a law requiring large frontier AI developers to submit to independent annual audits. Pritzker’s act received less fanfare—but it may ultimately matter more for the way AI risks are regulated around the world. The Illinois law joins earlier legislation passed in California and New York that, together, will impact every major AI developer in the United States. Collectively, these laws require AI companies to publicly document how they manage catastrophic risk, report safety incidents to state authorities, and, with Illinois’s new legislation, open their safety frameworks to external scrutiny. These efforts show that, even without any comprehensive federal statute or international agreement, U.S. states have the power to meaningfully regulate AI. Most frontier AI developers are headquartered in California, and California law applies to any company doing business there. As other states copy and strengthen Sacramento’s regulations, the rule sets will increasingly function as national, even in some respects international, standards. And they may prove more enforceable—and even more open to democratic pressure—than the global grand bargains experts call for. * China’s Quiet Bet on Methanol Is Paying Off
  Foreign Policy | 03 Aug 20:59 UTC
  An abandoned project reveals why industrial policy must preserve technological options.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. China is an electric vehicle giant, with companies such as BYD and Geely dominating global markets. But a lesser-known technology is also part of that clean transport story: methanol-powered flexible-fuel vehicles. That story begins with an almost forgotten collaboration, one powered by the U.S. motor giant Ford, that began in 1995. The partnership never produced a commercial breakthrough. Ford eventually abandoned methanol, while China moved on to other technologies. Yet nearly three decades later, methanol vehicles have reappeared in China, where Geely now operates one of the world’s largest fleets of methanol-powered commercial vehicles. China is an electric vehicle giant, with companies such as BYD and Geely dominating global markets. But a lesser-known technology is also part of that clean transport story: methanol-powered flexible-fuel vehicles. That story begins with an almost forgotten collaboration, one powered by the U.S. motor giant Ford, that began in 1995. The partnership never produced a commercial breakthrough. Ford eventually abandoned methanol, while China moved on to other technologies. Yet nearly three decades later, methanol vehicles have reappeared in China, where Geely now operates one of the world’s largest fleets of methanol-powered commercial vehicles. Tech leadership, especially in Big Tech forms of innovation, involves more than the technology itself. As the Ford story shows, a critical element is whether governments, firms, and local institutions are willing to sustain multiple technological pathways long enough for one of them to mature. Ford’s interest in methanol long predated its push into China. Methanol is a relatively convenient alternative fuel for conventional internal combustion engines. Existing gasoline engines can be modified to run on methanol blends or even pure methanol, and it can be produced from a wide range of feedstocks, from coal to biomass. The flexibility makes it particularly attractive for countries seeking greater energy security. Yet methanol has faced significant technical and commercial challenges as it has a lower energy density than gasoline, requiring larger fuel tanks for the same driving range; corrodes engines; and doesn’t perform well off a cold start. Ford had experimented with alternative fuels since the Model T , which could run on gasoline, ethanol, or kerosene. * Europe’s Dangerous Hesitation on Its Own Air Defense
  Foreign Policy | 03 Aug 11:31 UTC
  Russia’s ballistic missile attacks on Ukraine pose an urgent question for NATO leaders.
  FULL TEXT: Understanding the conflict four years on. U.S. President Donald Trump has now backed away from his previous hints that he might grant Ukraine a license to build Patriot interceptors. It also seems that Washington has declined Kyiv’s request for 300 such interceptors to bolster air defense. But even if these things were to happen, they would not solve Ukraine’s immediate problem: There are simply not enough interceptors to protect its cities from Russian ballistic missiles. It would take years to build new production facilities, and the Trump administration now seems to be unwilling to share sensitive interceptor technology, including seekers, solid rocket motors, and guidance software, with the Ukrainians. Nor would licensing resolve the underlying arithmetic. The United States produces a little more than 600 PAC-3 interceptors annually; the goal is to triple that number in the coming years. But Ukraine alone can burn through 150 to 180 in a single month of intensive Russian attacks. For the foreseeable future, Ukraine’s anti-ballistic missile defense will still depend on drawdowns from allied stockpiles and stopgap systems, complemented by ongoing Ukrainian and European projects to build alternatives to the Patriots . * A Currency Trader at Heart, Bessent Bets on Japan’s Yen
  NYT Economy | 04 Aug 04:00 UTC
  Coordinated intervention allowed Japan to support its currency without selling off its U.S. debt holdings.
  FULL TEXT: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent made $1 billion betting against the yen as the top investor for George Soros more than a decade ago. Now, he is using the might of the U.S. economy to prop up the Japanese currency. But this time, Mr. Bessent is doing so to help ensure that Washington’s borrowing costs do not creep even higher and to support a faltering American ally in China’s backyard. The coordinated effort by the United States and Japan to stabilize the rapidly weakening yen late last week marked the second time in the last year that the Treasury secretary returned to his roots as a hedge fund manager to make a big currency bet. The move demonstrated the lengths that the Trump administration is prepared to take to shield the U.S. economy from the volatility of global markets at a moment of political peril. The scale of the yen purchases was not disclosed. During a cabinet meeting on Friday, a photograph of Mr. Bessent’s note pad — which he has previously said he keeps visible for the media to peruse — showed purchases of between $5 billion and $10 billion worth of yen on his “to do” list. The Treasury Department’s most recent currency report, published last month, said the yen was substantially undervalued and that excess volatility in the currency’s trading was “undesirable.” Currency analysts said that the United States used its holdings of euros to buy yen, bolstering its value without shaking confidence in the dollar. In announcing the intervention on Sunday, Mr. Bessent called to expand the Federal Reserve’s repurchase agreement facility for foreign and international monetary authorities, which was established by Congress in 2020. It currently allows central banks that hold U.S. Treasuries to borrow up to $60 billion to bolster their holdings of dollars. We are having trouble retrieving the article content. * What Are Companies Getting for All That A.I. Spending?
  NYT Economy | 03 Aug 18:56 UTC
  A new field of “tokenomics” has emerged to measure the return on all the money companies are pouring into artificial intelligence.
  FULL TEXT: Corporate America was enthusiastic about artificial intelligence. Until it got the bill. Whiplash around spending on “tokens,” the units of computing power in which A.I. is sold, is hitting engineering teams and board rooms. First there was “ tokenmaxxing ,” as executives encouraged as much A.I. use as possible. Then there was “ tokenminning ,” after they rapidly burned through millions of dollars in company money. The simultaneous urgency and uncertainty is raising complex questions. What is A.I. even good for? How do you know what you’re buying, and measure the value of what it enables? How is it priced, and how will those prices change in the future? What does the spending on it displace? “It’s a currency where you have no instinct to know what you’re using, and the accounting practices aren’t even there for it,” said Howard Rubin, an economist who advises companies on technology spending. “The A.I. stuff is being treated as an investment right now, but it’s a risky investment in case it has no return.” The high stakes and scarcity of knowledge have given rise to a new field within economics devoted to figuring out how companies buy A.I. and what value they get from it. Call it tokenomics: the study of how this limited resource is created, traded and converted into things people want. Businesses are no strangers to adopting new technologies. Electricity allowed power to be generated far from where it was used. The internet radically changed how services are delivered. Cloud computing turned data processing from a clunky on-site task to something that could be rented remotely. The implications of A.I. could be as profound, but the costs and benefits are more difficult to weigh. We are having trouble retrieving the article content.   FULL TEXT: Hosted by Faisal Al Yafai Featuring Ryan Gingeras Produced by Finbar Anderson Listen to and follow The Lede Apple Podcasts | Spotify | Youtube | Podbean Historian Ryan Gingeras relishes an opportunity to discuss culture — especially when it intersects with the topic of his new book, “Mafia: A Global History.” In “The Godfather Part II,” Gingeras tells Faisal Al Yafai on The Lede, we see how “the American mafia is not only a critical element of the country’s history, it’s reflective of some of the more formative elements in the making of the modern U.S.” As demonstrated by his new book’s subtitle, while movies like “The Godfather” focus on criminal networks in the United States, such groups exist globally. Referencing one of the principal antagonists in the first “Godfather” film, he says, “The [Virgil] Sollozzo character in real life more likely would have had second, perhaps even thirdhand connections to the opium fields. The people who brought the refined morphine could have been Armenian, they could have been Lebanese, most likely Lebanese Christians. And the people who actually brought him the actual heroin, the dope, most likely would’ve been French.” Gingeras and Al Yafai’s discussion begins with American mafia culture, but expands both geographically and thematically, taking in how networks like those depicted on screen helped to build and shape the modern nations in which they now operate. Gingeras himself, an expert in Ottoman history, came to realize the personal impact of such networks when travelling to Turkey for work. “It took me leaving not so much New York, but leaving the United States and coming to Turkey to figure out that this was something that was a bigger story,” Gingeras says. “People all over the world struggled with this idea of, ‘What does the world mean to me when mafias are such an important part of it?’ In New York, it’s second nature, but what we, especially in the United States, oftentimes fail to really grasp is that this is something people struggle with everywhere.” Despite the fact they operate in the underworld, mafias matter to people everywhere, Gingeras argues, because they bring into relief the blurred edges of the nation-state. “Mafias are people with faces,” Gingeras says.

In Nigeria, PEPFAR funded nearly all — 99.9% — of the national budget for medicines used to treat and prevent HIV.   FULL TEXT: When Nancy Attia, 41, a restaurant owner, first saw the Egyptian doctor Diaa al-Awady in a video, she was “appalled by his aggressive attitude.” The man, who would go on to garner nearly 3 million followers on his Facebook page and become a viral sensation in the Arab world months after his death, was espousing a diet called “tayyibat” that would soon become something akin to an anti-scientific cult with adherents across the Middle East. She tried al-Awady’s diet after losing hope in the doctors she said had failed to address her illness. She had watched one of al-Awady’s videos in which he argued that iron supplements should be replaced by food items. It triggered a desire to stop taking the supplements that caused her severe side effects. For three months, she replaced them with al-Awady’s advised food plan — eating raisins, dates, blackstrap molasses and gluten-free, high-sugar foods. She refrained from eating poultry, eggs, milk and dairy, and had already restricted white flour before starting the diet.   FULL TEXT: When I arrived at the wedding, the bride was not the first thing that caught my eye. It was the portrait of a young man killed in the war, hanging behind the spot where the bride and groom would stand just minutes later. No one had taken it down or tried to conceal it behind the white fabric draped as a simple backdrop for the ceremony. It remained in place, framed by a few artificial flowers, as guests settled into plastic chairs and children darted between the tents nearby. In Gaza, joy no longer comes after mourning has ended. The two now exist side by side. A few minutes later, the bride entered to the sound of ululations. There was no long procession of cars, no brightly lit wedding hall like those that once hosted celebrations across Gaza. Instead, the family had carved out a small space inside a displacement camp, trying, if only for a few hours, to make it feel different from the rows of tents surrounding it. Halfway through the music, the speakers suddenly fell silent. It was not a power cut — Gaza has had virtually no electricity since the war began — but a drained battery powering the sound system. The celebration paused. For a brief moment, several guests instinctively looked up as the familiar buzz of an Israeli surveillance drone passed overhead. A few minutes later, one of the young men returned with another battery. The music resumed, and so did the ululations, as though these interruptions had become just another part of a Gaza wedding, no less familiar than the constant sound of drones. Watching the scene unfold, I realized this wedding was unlike any I had known before the war. It was not only the setting that had changed. The meaning of celebration itself had been transformed. Before the war, weddings in Gaza were planned months in advance. Families booked lavish wedding halls, prepared long guest lists and celebrated late into the night. If a close relative died, weddings were almost always postponed for months — sometimes for an entire year — in keeping with long-standing traditions of mourning passed down through generations. Today, after nearly three years of war, those customs belong to a life that can no longer be afforded. He was simply getting chemo as a safety measure, he told me, as his wife Yolanda worked on a laptop next to his bed. Outside this hospital ward, though, the prognosis for Lebanon isn’t quite so optimistic. A country of 6 million people, Lebanon has the fastest-rising rates of both new cancer cases and cancer deaths in the world, according to a study published in October 2025 by the leading United Kingdom-based medical journal The Lancet. From 1990 to 2023, cancer cases rose by 162%. Cancer-related deaths increased by 80%. Lebanon was once a beacon of healthcare in the Arab region. But the economy crashed in 2019 and hasn’t recovered since, sending doctors and nurses abroad for better jobs and making some medicines prohibitively expensive. Intense Israeli bombing campaigns on southern Lebanon and Beirut’s suburbs have also sent untold numbers of cancer patients fleeing for their lives, missing critical chemotherapy appointments, and at least one cancer ward suffered damage from the strikes, all of which can only worsen a worrying trend that was already in place even before the latest bloodshed. “Everyone abandoned me when I got cancer,” one young survivor, 32-year-old Layal Hachem, told me at a breast cancer rally in Beirut.
